Its bumper sits 7 cm further back, and its front indicators are amber lenses wrapping round the outer ends of the lamps (they were inside the bumper). Both have a repeater on each front wing.
- **The wheel and dash**: the wheel was inside the binnacle's hood. The dash is now shallower (its face 0.2 m behind the screen's foot), the column comes out of the face and the wheel's middle is about 0.4 m ahead of the hips and 0.33 m up; the shroud is small and low so the dials show over it. The Fiesta's hazard switch is on top of the column, as the Series III's; the Corsa's has no surround and is in the middle of the dash over the stereo, which moved there from the console in both. The Fiesta's choke is a pull knob just inside the wheel's rim. The stalk shows below the wheel now the column is clear of the dash. Amy's dash, binnacle and the panel round her dials are brown.
- **The plates** on the Fiesta's front bumper (number and L) stand proud of its chrome strip. **Belts** hang from the B pillars (the front) and the rear quarters (the back), with a mounting at the top. **The handbrake** pivots on top of the tunnel (it was inside it).
- **Jess's max-power bits**: a small sports wheel with a red top mark, a billet alloy knob with a red top, drilled alloy pedals, fluffy dice and the air freshener, a sun strip with JESS on it, a boot spoiler and a bass box in the boot.
- **Starters**: the BBC's starts catch within half a second, the Fiesta's interior crank was barely above the hiss, and the Carlton's fired part way through, so looped they stuttered and seemed to catch. Both cars now crank on a CC0 recording of a car that won't start (jerry.berumen, 511685): seven seconds of even cranking with a compression every 120 to 140 ms, each car's loop four steady compressions cut just ahead of one (the Fiesta about 220 rpm, the Corsa, from its second attempt, about 240), set about 10 dB under the idle (a new `oneshotGain` in the slicer, since one-shots don't take a source's gain). The crank now runs straight into the catch, with no 50 ms dead gap. Offline: cranking at about -33 dB against an idle of -22.
- **Frame time**: only the cars' outsides cast shadows now, and the tyres are lighter. The spawn view: 2,516 draw calls (budget 2,600; the last commit had 2,247), 3.98 M triangles (budget 3.5 M, but the last commit was already at 3.77 M before the cars), the game's own time 6 ms. Loading to Ready: 2.10 s.
- Checked: unit tests; screenshots from outside and from each driver's seat; a headless drive of each (starts, pulls away in first to about 17 mph and into second). Not checked: everything listed under the first pass's "Not checked", and how the hazard switch reads on top of the Fiesta's column (it may sit in the shadow of the wheel).

### The girls' cars, third pass (Jamie's list)

- **Everyone sat too far forward**, in the cars, both Land Rovers and the UTV: the hips were 0.23 to 0.28 m in front of the backrests' faces (a person's are about 0.12), which put the wheel against your chest and the mirror over your head. The Land Rovers' hips moved back 0.12 m (front to z -0.34, the 200TDi's back bench to -1.12, the cushions staying where they were), the UTV's 0.11 m (to -0.31), and the worn belts' chest and lap points with them (`SEAT_BACK` in each model), so the girls' poses and your eyes follow. The Land Rover's column is shorter (the wheel at 0.42 along it, was 0.5). The cars' front hips are about 0.3 m behind the screen's top (Fiesta z -0.27, Corsa -0.26) with the back seats 0.08 m further back, the wheel much closer to the dash (0.2 along the column, was 0.28), and the mirror lower on a stem, so it is ahead of you.
- **The Fiesta's hazard switch** couldn't be seen because its column points almost at your eye and the dials sit behind the wheel: anything on top of the column hid behind the wheel's boss, and "up" off the raked column also leans into the binnacle. It is on a pod standing up off the column just ahead of the wheel, where it shows over the boss, and the Fiesta's lamps moved into two short rows above it. Checked by screenshot from the seat.
- **The Fiesta's dials**: two the same size, the speedometer and a combined fuel and water gauge (as a Mk1 L's), with the lamps between, black rims, pale green-white figures and orange needles, set lower so the hood doesn't clip them.
- **Boots**: shorter again (Fiesta tail at -1.64, Corsa -1.68), the hatch glass coming down nearer the tail, and a shut line across the tail just above the bumper and up each side to the glass, with a lock, so the tailgate reads as one.
- **The Corsa**: its tail lamps are lower, all on the tailgate below the glass; the plate between them; the sun strip lies on the outside of the screen (it was turned edge-on); Jess's seats are tall buckets in a loud zigzag cloth, with side bolsters, the headrest built in and a harness slot (the old headrests floated off the backrests); it shakes less (vibration `steadiness` 0.82, `pulseGain` 0.75, less heave and pitch, half the random stumble, `erraticHunt` 0.02, less wear and roughness) and its springs are damped firmer (3.2 and 3).
- **Belts** have their top mountings inside the glass, where the pillars lean in; the back ones hang from the rear quarters ahead of the hatch (they stuck out of the doors and the back).
- **Mud**: a model's `cleanMaterials` (the seats, dash, carpet, door cards, headlining and belts) are left out of the mud coat, so the insides of the cars and the Land Rovers stay clean.
- **Parking**: the hard-standing is 8.4 m deep (was 6.8) and the cars 5.2 m apart (were 3.4), so a girl can walk between them.
- **The Fiesta's idle** is 830 to 870 warm (Jamie: about 850). Its recording idles at 1,084 to 1,187, so each beat is stretched to fill the longer cycle, to about 0.73 of its speed at the slowest, above the 0.45 where the sound would leave a gap (a test keeps it there).
- **The Fiesta breaking up as it revved** was a bug in every vehicle's sound: the game passed the engine's events to the sound without their strength, so an overrun pop or a spit-back had a loudness of NaN, which silenced the whole engine sound until it reset. The Fiesta pops often on the overrun, so it showed there. `engineAudio.js` and the render tool now pass the strength, and the worklet falls back to 0.5 if one ever comes without. Also, the Mini's blips had given the Fiesta's bank some grains a quarter or half the length of a cycle (13 to 20 ms, labelled up to 9,000 rpm); the slicer's period check now refuses them for both cars. Renders of the Fiesta, the Corsa, the red quad and the Series III: no NaN.
- **Tunable**: yes, as every vehicle; a FAQ answer says where.
- Not checked: the girls' poses in the moved seats (sitting, climbing in and out, the belts across them), the UTV and the Land Rovers from the seat after the move, and the cars on the move.

### The girls' cars, fourth pass (Jamie's list)

- **The windscreen's distance from your eye.** Measured the same way in each (horizontally, at eye height, at the same 1.15 scale): the Land Rovers' glass was 0.95 m ahead with the screen's top 0.92 m ahead; the Fiesta's 0.66 m with its top only 0.34 m ahead, the Corsa's 0.68 m with its top 0.16 m ahead and the mirror 0.09 m in front of your eye, so you couldn't see it. The cars' screens are less raked (the Fiesta 45 degrees from upright, was 54; the Corsa 49, was 60), the seats sit further back (front hips at z -0.4 and -0.34, the back seats at -1.1 and -1.14), with the B pillars and the roof's back edge moved back to match: the glass is now 0.92 and 0.94 m ahead, the screen's top 0.69 and 0.6, the mirror 0.62 and 0.53. `carEyeToGlass` and `landyEyeToGlass` measure it, and a test holds each car within 0.06 (model units) of the Land Rovers and the mirror at least 0.4 ahead of you.
- **The wheel and dash** for a driver sat further back: the dash is deeper (its face 0.32 m behind the screen's foot) and the column longer (each car's `wheelOut`), so the wheel's middle is about 0.5 m ahead of the hips without sticking far out. Jess's small wheel sits lower (`columnY` 0.575 against Amy's 0.71) with a smaller boss, so all her dials and lamps show over it. The pedals, handbrake and tunnel follow the seats. Checked from both seats.
- **The primer wing's grey line on the front**: its outline closed across the arch and ran on into the rounded nose. It now stops where the nose starts and runs round the arch.
- **Door handles**: the side details (handles, shut lines, rubbing strips, repeaters, mirrors) sat inside the Corsa's rounder side, whose bevel puts its face further out than the Fiesta's. They're placed on each car's own outer face now, and the Corsa's handles are dark grey pulls in a recess.
- **A boomier Corsa**: a new audio param, `pulseDrone` (dB) at `pulseDroneFreq` (Hz), a low boost on the pulse sound that is full at idle and half of it high up the revs. The Corsa has 9 dB at 75 Hz and `recVolume` 1.1: offline its idle is 3.7 dB louder, nearly all of it below 150 Hz, and full throttle half a dB. Renders at 6, 9 and 12 dB are in `.scratch/cars-sound/renders/` (`corsa-revs-drone*.wav`). A first go: more refinement later.


## The Corsa's dash and idle (TODO 54)

- Jamie: the Corsa's warning lights were hidden behind the wheel. A first go raised the binnacle and lowered the column; Jamie then found the column far too low and asked for the Fiesta's way instead: the dials seen through the hoop of the wheel. The column is back up (`columnY` 0.71, was 0.575) and `carDashThroughWheel` in `carModel.js` measures where the sight line from the driver's eye to the binnacle crosses the wheel's plane: the lamp strip at 0.062 above the middle (the boss reaches 0.035) and the top of the dials at 0.136 (the rim's inner edge is 0.15). A test holds both cars to that, so a change to the seat, wheel or dash that hides the dials fails. Not checked by screenshot.
- **Idle too high when warm**: the injection's fast idle (`coldIdleRpm` 450) fell away linearly with temperature, so at half way up the gauge the Corsa still idled about 225 rpm high and took the full 300 s to reach 880. It now falls with the cube of how cold it is (`engineModel.js`; only injected engines use it): about 1,110 rpm at 0.2, about 30 rpm over 880 by 0.6 and 7 by 0.75. Cold starts are as before.

## Hour meters in game time (TODO 35)

- **Hours are game hours.** `advanceUsage` (`src/vehicle/dashState.js`) multiplies the frame's seconds by a `timeScale`, which `Quad.updateDash` takes from `dashClock().rate()`; the game sets that to the day's `clockRate` (12 game seconds a real second by default), so five real minutes of running adds one hour, the same hour the dash clock shows. It follows the Tune tab's clock rate live.
- **Odometer and trip stay as they were.** They are distance, and the farm's metres are already the game's metres, so nothing scales them.
- **Starting readings and saves are unchanged.** The ranges in the specs were already meant as hours of use, so they now sit in proportion to how much a game adds. Wear and faults are tied to the readings at the start only, not to hours added during play.
- **Not checked:** the feel of the numbers over a long run in the browser; unit test only.

## A quad towing a quad (TODO 39)

Settled with Jamie before building: a rope that stretches (not the chain), taken and hooked as the chain is, either girl steers the dead quad when asked, her braking a simple controller with a per-girl difference, and no snapping in this first version.

- **The rope** (`src/items/towRope.js`, `ropeForce`, `ropeLengthFor` and `isSnatch` pure and tested). `X` on foot looking at a red or blue quad takes it off the rack (the green's `X` is its winch; the tractor and the cars have their own ways), `E` on a dead quad hooks it, `E` again unhooks, `X` puts it back. It is hooked with 0.3 m of slack, kept between 2.5 and 5 m long. Once tight it is a spring and damper: 5,000 N/m plus 900 N s/m while it is lengthening, at most 5,000 N. The damping is what makes a snatch: pulling off hard takes up the slack at speed and the dead quad is jerked. A tow quad of about 290 kg feels the whole pull back (`reaction` 1), unlike the chain, which drags a 2,000 kg tractor a quarter. The hooked quad is `towed` (brakes off), as with the chain.
- **A snatch** is the rope going tight at 1.8 m/s of lengthening or more (`snatchRate`). The game passes it on as a drive event to the girl steering, who says `towSnatch`.
- **Steering it** (`src/npc/towSteer.js`, tested). `TowSteer` has the shape of `HerDrive` (`controls`, `update`, `stop`, `done`, `result`) so the driving state runs it in place of her own driving; no engine is started and nothing is chosen. The bars aim at the towing quad's hitch. The brake is from time to contact: the room between the hitches (less 0.4 m for the quads' ends) divided by how fast it is closing, against her horizon (`towHorizon`: Amy 2.6 s, Jess 1.2 s, 1.8 default). There is a touch of brake on a slack rope she is closing on, and a hold while the towing quad is stopped and she is close. Jess looks away now and then (`towDistractChance` 0.04 a second against Amy's 0.004, for 1.6 s): no steering, no braking, which is how she runs up into your back. A real collision does the rest, as the dead quad has a collider. Running into it at 1.2 m/s or more gets `towBump`. She gets off by herself when the rope comes off (`towDone`), or after 25 s with nobody on the towing quad. She does not drive the dead quad home afterwards (`towing` is checked in `endDrive`).
- **Asking her** is on her wheel: "Steer the towed quad" when the rope is on a quad nobody is on, it is still, and she could sit on it; "Get off the towed quad" while she is on it. Debug: "Hook the tow rope on it / take it off" on the Vehicles tab (from the nearest utility quad within 8 m) and a "tow rope" line in Running; the Girls tab's "steer-tow"; the console's `vehicle.<id>.towRope` and `towRope`; two Tune groups, "Tow rope" and "Girl steering a tow".
- **Left alone:** snapping, a girl driving the towing quad while the player steers the dead one, towing uphill specially, and the rope on the UTV or the Land Rovers. The trailer and the rope can't share a quad's hitch (each says so).
- **Checked:** unit tests (rope force and hooking with stand-in quads, the controller and the driver). **Not checked:** how the rope looks, how the dead quad behaves with real physics on the end of it (the stiffness and damping are sensible guesses and unmeasured), a girl walking to and sitting on a quad that is being pulled, and no headless run was done.

## A worn clutch on the manual quads (TODO 50)

Settled without asking, as the item was clear.

- A tenth fault, `clutch`, in `faults.js`, on the four manual quads only (`hasClutch`, set in `quad.js` from having a gearbox and not being the tractor, a Land Rover, a car or the UTV; history only deals it to the same). Unlike the others it starts and worsens from how the clutch is used, not from running time: `clutchSlipping` (pure, tested) gives 0 to 1 for the pedal at the bite (clutch 0.05 to 0.85), in gear with the engine running, and either loaded (throttle 0.3 or more, or a climb of sine 0.05 or more) or creeping below 2 m/s in first or second. It is strongest in the middle of the bite. Half-holding the pedal while rolling fast on the flat with no throttle counts for nothing.
- Starts after about 400 s of full slipping (`clutchEvery`, scaled by the quad's own luck like the rest), then takes about 500 s more of slipping to go from 0.15 to worn out (`clutchSeconds`); it does not get worse unless slipped.
- Effect (`clutchSlipUnderPower`): nothing until wear passes 0.2, then slip grows with wear and with throttle. In `quad.js` that slip takes up to 75% off the drive (`clutchDriveLoss`), is fed to the engine's existing `slip` control (the belt's flare, so the revs run away and the load stops holding them down) and loosens the engine's tie to the road (`gearLock` times one minus the slip). So it is the belt's symptom by another cause.
- Bench only (`BENCH_ONLY_KINDS`): "Fit a new clutch plate", the sixteenth key (the quote), with an inspect step (`clutchLook`). Done badly it leaves a fault at the usual botched level. No spare part. The girls mention it (`faultClutch`, `histClutch`, which says it smells hot) while the quad is ridden. Tests in `tests/clutchWear.test.js`.
- Left out: a real smell or smoke from the hot clutch (only the girls' words), the clutch plate on the tractor, Land Rovers and cars, and a clutch cooling down. Not checked in the running game: how quickly it wears in play (the numbers are first guesses) and how the flare feels.

## Starting a cab from the seat (TODO 63)

- A girl sent to a Land Rover that is not running (the cold-day warm-up, the rain, the warm-up sit; not a sulk's walk, which is the same path) now climbs in first and starts it from the seat. `startFromSeat` / `runSeatStart` in `companionHayRide.js` run the ordinary `StartingAttempt` (key, choke, starter, minding the choke until `settleAt`) while she is in `onTrailer`, so her lines and the choke habits are the same as outside.
- A flat battery still sends her to the starting handle outside (`needsStartingHandle` in `crankAttempt.js`, shared with `needsHandle`), because the handle is at the front bumper. Nothing to swing from a seat.
- If it will not start she drops the shelter and climbs back out. Moved off the seat part-way (you take the vehicle), the attempt is abandoned.
- Not checked in the running game: the look of her reaching the choke from the passenger bench (she uses the sitting pose, hands in her lap). Unit-tested: the handle-or-seat rule.

## Odometer drums that roll

- The mechanical counters (odometer, trip and hour meter drums on the pods and the older dashes) now turn instead of changing digit. `drumPositions` (`src/vehicle/dashState.js`, pure, tested) gives each drum a digit plus how far it has rolled: the tenths drum moves steadily with the distance, and each drum above it moves only while the one below goes from 9 to 0, the way a real carry does. `counterDigits` returns that as `pos`, and a `key` (positions to a sixth of a digit) for the faces' change gates, so a face redraws about once a second at speed rather than every frame. `drum` in `dashParts.js` draws the current digit sliding up out of its window with the next under it, and a little shading top and bottom. The LCD dashes (`counterText`) are unchanged.
- Not checked in the running game: only the unit tests ran, so how it looks on each dash is unseen.

## Shape checks for the models (TODO 86)

The vehicles were already covered by item 87's tests in `tests/vehicleModels.test.js` (triangle budgets, fixed points, proportions). What was missing was the girls, so `tests/girlModels.test.js` builds each one in node (`tests/helpers/girlModels.js`: the real `companion.glb`, her figure and her outfit) and checks her mesh count exactly, her triangles within 5% and her bounding box within 3% of recorded numbers, and that she is about as tall as her params say. Amy is 7 meshes, about 2720 triangles, 1.61 m tall; Jess the same meshes, 1.55 m. The test has no texture pixels, so the polo's neckline is not cut and the triangle count runs a little above the game's; the numbers are for catching accidents, not for the budget. To change a girl on purpose, update `WAS` in the test and say why here.

## A shared detail kit for the vehicle models (TODO 87)

Settled with Jamie before building:

- Scope: every piece of the kit is built in `src/vehicle` with tests where it's pure. The new tread and the wear layer (with dried mud in the nooks) go on every vehicle in this item. Bolts, cables and hoses, discs and calipers, and decals wait for the per-vehicle items (88 to 101), which say where each goes.
- Wear: a `wear` amount on each spec (0 new, 1 derelict), set from what the per-vehicle items say: heavy on the rusty quad and the ATC, worn on the red and the green, lighter on the blue and the orange, 0 on the loan. The later items may adjust it.
- How wear is drawn: per-vertex "edge" (sharp convex corners, where paint scuffs) and "nook" (concave corners, where rust and dried mud collect) worked out from the geometry when it's built, and a shader layer that blends scuffs, fading and rust from them. It joins the mud coat, so mud sits in the nooks first. No UVs or textures.
- Decal names: invented makers, never a real name or logo. Kestrel (the red quad), Tamura (the blue and the loan), Ridgeback (the green), Viper (the yellow and the orange), Hartley (the tractor), Moorland (the Land Rovers), Thorne (the Fiesta), Ashby (the Corsa). The kit takes any text, so later items can change them.
- Triangle budgets, checked in a test: a quad 65k, the bike and the ATC 35k, the tractor 70k, the UTV 80k, the Land Rovers and cars 100k, a trailer 15k. Measured before the kit: quads 38k to 46k, tractor 41k, UTV 53k, Series 57k, Defender 70k, Fiesta 45k, Corsa 57k, trailers 4k to 6k. At the budgets the vehicles add at most about 0.4M triangles to the spawn view's 2.8M.
- Checking: unit tests (including that seat, grip, footrest, dash and attach points don't move) and one headless load for shader errors. Jamie looks at it and gives feedback, rather than screenshot rounds.
- Jamie, on starting: items 88 to 101 should leave each vehicle much more in proportion and much more like its real counterpart than now. So the kit carries a table of real sizes for each vehicle (wheelbase, track, length, width, height, seat height, tyre size), and a proportion check a vehicle item opts its vehicle into once done, so it can't drift back. The fixed points (seat, grips, footrests, dash, attach points) still stay put unless a vehicle item finds one badly out of proportion; then it moves it on purpose and checks the girls' poses, rather than leaving the body wrong round it.
- Who builds what: this item (the kit) by the strongest model; 88 to 101 can go to a cheaper one, one vehicle at a time with Jamie looking at each, following the "Using the detail kit" section in CLAUDE.md.
- Built (`src/vehicle/detail/`, with tests in `tests/tread.test.js`, `wearBake.test.js`, `decals.test.js`, `detailKit.test.js`, `realSizes.test.js`, `vehicleModels.test.js`):
  - **Tread** (`tread.js`): eight kinds, each a carcass profile and a block layout: `atv` (chevrons meeting in a V, shoulder blocks), `farm` (square blocks in three rows), `knobbly`, `balloon`, `tractor` (R1 bar lugs, open centre), `rib` (tractor front), `allTerrain`, `road` (grooves and shoulder sipes). The whole tyre is scaled so its outermost point is exactly the wheel radius, so the physics still matches. Every vehicle's tyres now use it (the trailers' are item 101). About 0.6 ms a tyre to build, 2k to 4k triangles each; the quads went up about 2.5k triangles, the Defender to 82k.
  - **Wear** (`wear.js`, shader in `effects/wearLayer.js`, joined into `mudCoat.js`): each side of each triangle is marked convex, concave or not a crease (faces at least 20 degrees apart whose normals are split there, so a smooth cylinder's sides aren't creases but its rims are), and each vertex carries its distance to the opposite side, so the shader knows exactly how far any point is from an edge however few triangles there are. That needs each triangle's own vertices, so a creased geometry is un-indexed: about twice the vertices, packed small (creases as bytes, distances as 16-bit), about 1.1M vertices and 38 MB more GPU memory across ten vehicles. Nooks between separate parts come from other parts' points within 3 cm in front of a vertex. The kind of wear follows the material: rubber perishes grey, bare metal rusts in the folds and shines on rubbed edges, painted metal chips to grey and rusts, plastic fades chalky and whitens where scuffed, with grime in its folds; `userData.metalBody` on the tractor, Land Rovers and cars makes their coloured panels painted metal. Mud gathers in nooks first (`cover += nook * (0.9 * mud + 0.6 * wear)`), so an old vehicle has dried mud in them however clean.
  - **Baking in the background**: about 40 ms a vehicle for the creases and 100 to 150 ms for the nooks in node, too much for the loading budget (2.44 s against 2.5), so `game.bakeWear` does 2 ms a frame while playing and 12 ms behind the start card: all ten vehicles were done 7 s after the page loaded. Until then a vehicle shows only the fading. The barn find's `weatherModel` used to drop the mud coat's shader patch when it cloned the materials (so it never got muddy); it keeps it now, and the find's `bodyWear` is 0.95.
  - **Decals** (`decals.js`): badge (italic, block or oval by maker), stripes that break up towards the back, the yellow warning label, a riveted chassis plate with a seeded number, race number boards and a dealer's sticker (Pennine Farm Machinery). `MAKER_FOR` says whose badge each vehicle carries (the UTV is Ridgeback, the green's maker, as Kawasaki makes the Mule). A test fails if any maker, line or printed string has a real maker's name in it. Decals take the mud and the wear like any part, so a sticker's edges lift.
  - **Fixings** (`fixings.js`): hex, flange, dome, nut and allen heads with or without washers, placed in a circle or along a row and merged into one mesh. **Cables** (`cables.js`): a smooth path through clip points that sags between them, swept into a cable, or a hose (ribbed if asked) with a clamp at each end. **Brakes** (`brakes.js`): a disc (plain or petal) with drilled holes and hat bolts, a one- or two-piston caliper over its edge, and a finned drum with its backing plate, arm and cable stop.
  - **Real sizes** (`realSizes.js`): `REAL_SIZES` (the real counterpart's length, width, height, wheelbase, track and tyre, from makers' figures as best known: each vehicle item checks its own) and `measureModel` (the body without its wheels, the wheels where the layout puts them, ground at y = 0), compared as shape over the wheelbase so the game's 1.15 scale doesn't matter. `IN_PROPORTION` lists the vehicles a detail item has finished; the test holds each within 8%. Out of proportion now (over the wheelbase): every quad 15 to 20% too long and 11 to 16% too wide (the orange 32% too long), the ATC 21% too wide, the moto's tyres 12% small, the tractor 37% too wide and 43% too tall (its exhaust stack and bale spike frame are counted as body; check what the real figure includes), the Land Rovers about 20% too wide (and the 200TDi's wheelbase is the Series' 88 inches, not a 90's 2,360 mm), the Fiesta and Corsa 21% too wide and 6 to 10% too tall. The UTV is within 8% on all but height (17%).
  - **Budgets and fixed points**: `budgets.js` holds the triangle budgets; `tests/vehicleModels.test.js` checks them for every vehicle and both trailers, and checks 22 kinds of fixed point (seats, grips, footrests, pegs, dash, cameras, rider and pillion anchors, rack, mug, hitch, winch) against `tests/fixedPoints.json` to 2 mm. Moving one on purpose: check the girls, regenerate with `UPDATE_FIXED_POINTS=1`, and say why here.
- **The red quad as a Honda Foreman (TODO 88, done).** Reworked from photos Jamie put in `.scratch/quads/kestral/`. The decks and flares are sculpted in `src/vehicle/foremanDeck.js` (pure geometry, tested): a red deck across the front and another across the back, crowned, with the outer edge rolled down, the end rolled over into a face, the inner end swept in and the face's lower corners angled in, so there is no straight edge; one black flare ribbon follows each deck's outer edge (a strip across the top, a lip, and a skirt to the wheel arch that sweeps down into a tail at the inner end and tapers away round the face's corner), and each wing has a red inner wall just inboard of the tyre so the arch is a closed tunnel. The flares stop short of the tyres so the front of the front tyre and the back of the rear one show; twin rectangular headlights close to the middle with a two-slat body-coloured grille between them; a red dome and shroud round the steering column; red side panels under the seat closing the body between the decks; a tail lamp on a bracket under the rack and a tow plate bolted to the rails below it. The frame's rails end under the rear deck, and the racks stand on the decks at their outer edges, so no bar ends in the air. `REAL_SIZES.red` track is 880 mm (the old 830 did not fit the 1,105 mm width). `red` is in `IN_PROPORTION`: length +5%, width +7%, track +6%, height +5%, tyre -5% over the wheelbase. **Fixed points moved on purpose:** the red's handlebars, grips, dash and rider hands are 40 mm lower (bars at 0.96 m, not 1.0), at Jamie's say-so from the photos; the other quads are unchanged. The pillion's seat gets patchy grime like the rider's through `userData.wearNookMin` on a mesh (a floor for the wear bake's nooks, `wear.js`), since the rider's marks come from its seat being squeezed against the pillion's.
  - **The detail** (`src/vehicle/foremanDetail.js`, from the kit): rack weld beads at every join and two tie-down hooks on each outer rail; the grille between the headlights is a housing stood out of the face (the deck's face is one surface, with nothing cut from it) holding the oil cooler's finned core, a five-bladed fan and the two body-coloured slats; a low valance under the face so the front shocks and arms show; diamond tread plates bolted to the footboards in place of the grip ribs (the footwell meshes and their fixed points are unchanged); a shift lever (`gearLever`, so it wears like a knob) from the left of the engine to a rubber peg by the footboard; brake and throttle cables from the levers down the column to the shroud, children of the bars so they turn with them; rear drum brakes (the drum turns, the plate stays), fitted only to the Foreman's rear wheels; grey tape over a split in the rider's seat, tilted with it; the maker's badge ("Kestrel Farm 300") on each side panel, a millimetre off the surface, and "KESTREL" on the instrument cluster's face (drawn in `dash.js`). No number plate on the back: it would never leave the farm (Jamie).
  - **The red's bar controls** (`src/vehicle/foremanControls.js`, from Jamie's photo of the real switch pod): a black pod on the left bar, face to the rider, with a yellow LIGHTS rocker (OFF at the top, ON at the bottom), a white HI / LO rocker, a red RUN / OFF engine-stop rocker, a round start button and a ridged black CHOKE slider in a slot, all lettered on the face; a tapered brake lever with a ball end on each side, the left one the parking brake with a latch that swings out of a slot in its perch while it holds; and the 2WD / 4WD rocker on the right bar in a bezel with the two positions printed on the housing (4WD towards you). Parts the game poses keep their names (`chokeLever`, `parkBrakeLever`, `parkBrakeCatch`, `driveSwitchRocker`); the pod's own are `lightsRocker`, `beamRocker`, `stopRocker`, `startButton`, posed by `poseBarPod`. A new choke style, `slide`, for the red (same reach as the thumb lever: always in reach): the slider moves along its slot, pulled to the left to apply. **Lights on the red are now a switch** (`barLights` on the spec; `P` cycles dipped, main beam, off; it starts dipped so nothing changes until you touch it): the headlight still follows the engine as before, the switch turns it off (tail lamp too) or to main beam (`lightLook('high')`: the longer, whiter, brighter beam of the UTV's, at the brightness the engine gives it, and no extra battery load). Girls driving it leave it as you left it. The Vehicles tab and the console's `lights` set it. The red stop rocker only shows RUN while the key is on, and the start button goes in while you hold `R`: neither adds a way to fail to start. **Choke knobs are black everywhere** on the quads (they were yellow; yellow is the lights rocker's), with a small white mark; the Land Rovers' red knob and the cars' are left as they were. The red also has a proper thumb throttle (`thumbThrottle`: a housing with a cable adjuster and a curved paddle with a ridged pad under the right grip, pushed forward by the throttle; the other quads' stay the old block). "KESTREL" sits in the cluster's window under the hour and odometer drums, and the left brake cable runs ahead of and over the pod, never through it. Only the red has the new controls; the other quads' levers and switches are for later items. The front shocks and CV boots were already there. Boots' wear: the pillion's touch patches (which showed nothing on black vinyl) became two scuff patches on the side panels' lower edges; touch points are capped at eight in all, so check the count when adding one. Not done: oil-cooler hoses (the deck hides them) and a pose for the shift lever (it is for show; gears are still on the keys).
- Checked: unit tests; one headless load (`.scratch/pp/wearbake.mjs`): no shader errors, all ten vehicles baked 7 s after load, 19 wear programs compiled; loading 2.48 to 2.60 s across three runs (it was 2.44 at the last measure; the tyres add about 10 ms, the rest is run to run). Not checked: how any of it looks (Jamie looks); frame time with the extra vertices (`perf.mjs` not run); decals, bolts, cables and brakes aren't on any vehicle yet, so they are only checked by their tests.
- **The blue quad as a Grizzly 660 (TODO 89, done).** From six photos Jamie put in `.scratch/quads/tamura-660/` (front, rear, a three-quarter, the brochure shot, the left switch pod and the LCD). The blue gets its own body style, `grizzly` (the loan keeps `utility2` for its own item, 96). `REAL_SIZES.blue` is now the Grizzly (2,080 x 1,180 x 1,115, wheelbase 1,250, track 926, tyre 635; it was the Kodiak) and `blue` is in `IN_PROPORTION`: length +4%, width +5%, height +5%, track +5%, tyre -5% over the wheelbase. **No fixed point moved**: the bars' height was already within tolerance, so `fixedPoints.json` is untouched.
  - **Body** (`src/vehicle/grizzlyBody.js`): the red's deck machinery (`foremanDeck.js`, now taking a deck table) with the Grizzly's numbers: a front face 27 cm deep that carries the headlights at its corners and the grille at the middle, a higher rear deck, fenders painted all over (the red has black flares; the arch trim is black). `foremanFenders` takes the deck table and the flare material, so the two quads share it. The side panels and the cover between the decks are black plastic. The racks stand on the decks at their outer edges with straight legs, the rear one braced to the deck's back edge; their heights are the fixed `rackAnchor` and `mugAnchor` ones. The frame's rear cross tube ends at -0.84, inside the deck, so it does not show above the tail lamp.
  - **Detail** (`src/vehicle/grizzlyDetail.js`, from the kit): the steel bumper (a cross bar, two uprights from black mounts bolted on the front rack's rail, wings back to the fender faces, black end blocks bolted to the faces), a skid plate, a grille in plain boxes (the kit's rounded grille cost 3,000 triangles a quad, over the budget), the maker's badge, a tail lamp panel with reflectors and a rear beam, the "Bruin 660" stickers on the tank's sides and warning labels on the rear fender, front discs and calipers, silver wheels with five slots, `treadTyre` ATV tread. 62,000 triangles against 65,000.
  - **Controls** (`src/vehicle/grizzlyControls.js`, from the pod photo): a black pod face to the rider with a grey dotted LIGHTS rocker, HI, LO and OFF printed beside it, a step with the engine-stop symbols, a red stop switch and a grey start button. The levers, the 2WD / 4WD switch and the thumb throttle are the red's (`foremanControls.js`), with the blue's red diff lock button between the switch and the throttle. `poseThumbThrottle` now runs for every quad (it skips any without a moving lever), so the blue's throttle moves. **Left as it was:** the real pod has the choke lever on it, but the blue's choke is a plunger on the tank (a decision from before), so it is not drawn; and the pod's rockers do not move, because the blue has no `barLights` (the red's `P` switch is not on it). Both are easy follow-ups if wanted.
  - Looked at in headless renders from the front, rear, sides, above and the seat. The tests cannot see lettering; the pod's and the sticker's were read at close range.
  - **Jamie's first round of changes** (same item): the blue's choke moved off the tank to the pod's own lever (`chokeStyle: 'thumb'`, so it is always in reach; the lever hangs from the pod's underside and swings towards the rider to apply, posed by the existing thumb branch in quad.js). `barLights: true` on the blue, so `P` cycles off, dipped and main beam as on the red; the pod has one three-position rocker (HI top, LO middle, OFF bottom), so `poseBarPod` drives the lights rocker alone when there is no HI / LO one. The headlights are round lamps in a round black surround (they were round lamps on black rectangles). The dash is redrawn from the LCD photo: cyan backlight, one big speed, the odometer (alternating with the trip) and the hours, a thermometer and a fuel symbol, the L / H / R / P lamp column (forward lights H, reverse R, neutral P), a DIFF.LOCK lamp from `state.diffLock`, two round buttons and a made-up round badge; the clock and the fuel and temperature bars are gone (the real one has none). The racks are a powder-coat material (`metalness` 0.2), so the wear layer fades and grimes them and does not rust every weld (bare steel's `metalness` 0.45 counts as painted steel). The slit between the blue curved walls and the wings was the bulkhead ending 6 mm inside the skirt (`xOut` is now the skirt's own x, which also closes it on the red) plus the black side panels ending exactly where the wall's fillet starts: the Grizzly's run 25 mm into the walls.
  - **Second round**: the choke lever moved to the pod's right, clear of the stop and start buttons it swung across at full choke. The dash housing is 30 mm wider so the round badge sits beside the LCD, not on it; the DIFF.LOCK lamp is a lamp in a chamfered window with its name in bold above the LCD. The headlights are a bare round lens (no bezel, a clear grey lens you can see the dish through) in an angled black wedge on the face (`grizzlyLampOutline`), moved 15 mm inboard (then another 25 mm, at Jamie's say-so, to x 0.33, then to 0.26 to match the red's 0.25), with the bumper's end blocks lowered to 70 mm tall so their tops are below the wedges' bottoms. The wedge's top stays on the flat of the face: above it the deck rolls away.
  - **Third round**: the headlight surround is now an almond lying on its side, traced from a crop of the front photo: close round the lens at the inner end, the top arching over it and the bottom curving under it and the outer end sweeping up to a long point out towards the fender's top corner (`grizzlyLampOutline`, a closed spline). The wedge in the second round was not angled enough, and the first almond pointed down: the real one points up.
- **The green quad as a Kawasaki KLF300 Bayou (TODO 90, done).** From seven photos Jamie put in `.scratch/quads/ridgeback-quad/` (three-quarter, front, side, rear from above, the left switch housing and the speedo). The green gets its own body style, `bayou` (the old `utility3` stays for the three-wheeler, which borrows its bars). Answers to the questions up front: keep the winch; the choke becomes a slider (`chokeStyle: 'slide'`, like the red's: a knob suited nothing in the photos); the lettering is made up ("Ridgeback" on the grille and tank cover, "RB300", "Farmhand" in script and "4x4" on the side panels); no number plate and no dealer sticker; the paint is `0x33692c` (the old deep green nudged a shade towards olive; Jamie preferred ours to the photos' brighter one). **No fixed point moved.**
  - **Proportion.** `REAL_SIZES.green` is now 2,060 long (the book 1,930 plus the winch's fairlead and hook and a tow drawbar, which the farm's one wears) and 870 track (the old 840 left the shared layout's track 8.3% over); `green` is in `IN_PROPORTION`: length +5%, width +6%, height -0.5%, track +5%, tyre -7% over the wheelbase. The old model was 17% too long and 12% too wide (the bumper and rack stuck out).
  - **Body** (`src/vehicle/bayouBody.js`): the red's deck machinery with the Bayou's numbers (a flatter front deck, a higher rear one, black rubber flares over painted decks, a black tank cover, painted side panels). The nose cage is grey steel (`cageMaterial`, `metalness` 0.2 so the wear layer fades it and does not rust every weld): one bent loop for the top rail, uprights and bottom rail, a loop under and beside each lamp, and straight struts to the rack's front rail, the deck's face and the frame. `cagePieces` lists every bar and a test checks each ends on another bar, the rail, the face or the frame, and that the lamps sit inside their loops.
  - **Detail** (`src/vehicle/bayouDetail.js`): two square lamps in black housings and a slatted grille with the maker's badge on the face; the winch (the model's own drum, rope, motor and hook, still at the fixed fairlead and hook points) closed in with a cover, sides, a back plate and a four-roller fairlead; a tail panel and cross beam; a tow hitch with a drawbar, a hole through it, a pin dropped in with a head and a linch pin; the stickers; the cracked mudguard (a jagged dark line on the left rear flare's outer face with three cable ties across it) and oil weeping on the left crankcase. The weeping is on the case's outer face under the side panel and I could not find a view in which it shows, so it is there but easy to miss. Small boxes use one-segment rounded boxes: the first pass was 66,500 triangles against 65,000.
  - **Bars and speedo.** The bars rise in a straight V at about 20 degrees from a low middle (`bayou` in `buildHandlebars`), through the lever perch and the grips' fixed points, so the grips are where they were and only the bar under them changed; the pod, perches and throttle housing sit on the line. The speedo (`buildBayou` in `dash.js`) is a round cup on a bracket over the clamp, at the dash's fixed point: white mph to 40 round the edge (220 degrees, 20 at the top, `bayouNeedleAngle`, tested), a blue km/h scale inside it, a six-drum odometer, N and R lamps and the key in the empty sector under the hub, the maker's name from `makerBadge`. It follows the road speed like the red's.
  - **Pod** (`src/vehicle/bayouControls.js`, from the housing photo): a black housing, a wide black lights rocker, a red OFF / RUN rocker, a big red ENGINE STOP paddle and, on a foot under it, the red's slider (`chokeLever`, `baseX`, `POD.slide`), so the quad's existing slide pose works. The lights rocker and stop are not posed because the green has no `barLights`; the choke and the throttle are.
  - Looked at in headless renders (seed 3, the only seed here that deals a green with the rest: the fleet is dealt at random): nose, sides, rear, the bars and the speedo from the seat, the pod and the decals at close range.
  - **Jamie's first round of changes** (same item), from the photos again: (1) the back has two tail lamps, one each side, and (2) the rear face leans, out at the bottom and in at the top (`lean` on a deck spec in `foremanDeck.js`, 9 cm over the face, pushing the face rows back by how far down the face they are; the lamps sit on it through `rearFaceAt` in `bayouBody.js`, which reads the deck's own rows for the height and the tilt, about 23 degrees); (3) the front is barer: the body's face is only 10 cm deep, stopping high (its lip at 0.64), and the lamps, grille and radiator shroud stand 11 cm out of it on the cage, hung by short brackets from the cage's top rail and sill and stayed to the cradle, so you see through the nose; the winch housing stands on two mounts to the frame; (4) the speedo is lower and nearer the middle of the bars, which **moves the green's `dash` fixed point** (y 1.115 to 1.05, z 0.51 to 0.50; `fixedPoints.json` regenerated, nothing else changed); (5) `barLights: true` on the green, so `P` works the lights rocker as on the red and blue; (6) the N and R lamps and the key moved in from the bezel; (7) the side panels are short strips under the seat with an arch under them, so the left case cover shows, and the weeping is on its face (the earlier version was on the crankcase behind it, which that cover hides), in wet amber so it reads against the black. The side stickers moved up into the strip.
  - **Jamie's second round** (same item): the tail lamps moved out to the back's outer edges (`BAYOU_TAIL_X` 0.43, still on the leaning face through `rearFaceAt`), and the lights rocker rocks left to right (OFF left, dipped middle, main beam right) about its vertical like the stop rocker, flagged by `userData.sideways` so `poseBarPod` turns it about y; the red's and blue's still rock up and down.
- **The yellow quad as a Blaster-style sport quad (TODO 91, first try).** From three photos Jamie put in `.scratch/quads/tamura-blaster/` (a front-on shot, a three-quarter, the brochure's rear three-quarter) and a close-up of the carb's left side. Answers up front: the stock look (the photos' colours ignored: yellow plastics, black seat, silver frame, yellow springs); no race number plate; the dash is the key and nothing else, not even a lamp (`buildBlaster` in `dash.js` is now the kids' quad's small key housing, at the same fixed dash point); the maker is **Tamura**, as the blue and the loan (the badge is `Tamura Hornet 200`, after the Bruin 660 and the Blaster's 195 cc), so `MAKER_FOR.yellow` is `tamura` and Viper is the orange's alone; the choke and the fuel tap move to the side of the carb. The throttle is shown as a cable from the right grip down the right of the tank into the carb's slide cap (my reading of "add in the throttle").
  - **What Jamie asked to see:** the wheels are the edge of the quad, the body is smaller, and more engine shows. So every panel is narrower than the tyre beside it (`BLASTER_FENDERS`: fronts to x 0.585 against the tyre's 0.59, rears to 0.60 against 0.625; a test checks each fender against the tyre), the front fenders are small caps and the rear ones broad wings that stop near the tyres' ends, there are no side panels between them, and the cylinder is slimmer (fins 0.22 wide, not 0.29) so the barrel, crankcase, carb and frame show from the side. The old nose cowl, number plate and its backing, the black lamp pod and the tail cowl are gone; a small nose with a rectangular lamp high on its face and a four-slat mesh vent under it, a tail between the rear wings and two flat side plates under the seat (the badge goes on them) replace them.
  - **Own body style.** `blaster` in `quadModel.js` (the kids' quad keeps `sport`, which the yellow shared): fenders are `blasterFenders` (shells with the front's and rear's own profile and width, `blasterBody.js`), the engine takes a `blaster` flag (slimmer barrel, no carb or airbox of its own), and `blasterDetail.js` adds the nose, tail, bumper, footrests, the engine bay and the decals. The frame is silver (every tube the shared builders made in the black frame material is swapped for `silverMaterial()` in this model only), the springs yellow, the rims silver steel with six holes.
  - **Engine bay** (`addBlasterEngine`): the carb behind the cylinder (a mixing chamber, a slide cap, a float bowl with a drain screw, a flange), a clamped rubber boot to the airbox under the seat (it was hidden before), a recoil starter housing on the right of the crankcase, the fuel hose from the tap round the cylinder's left to the float bowl, and the throttle cable. **The choke** stays the `plunger` style (a hand off the bars, all but stopped to reach, the existing `chokeReach`) rather than a new one: its knob is black with the choke symbol (a ring with a bar, as geometry) and sits on a housing on the carb's left; it hangs from a group turned a quarter about z (`buildCarbPlunger`), so the quad's existing pull (down its own y) goes out along x, and a test checks that. **The tap** is a new `TAP_STYLES` place, `carbLeft`: a black knob on a body at the tank's lower left edge, in the sediment bowl's usual place, a hose from it to the carb.
  - **Footrests and bumper.** The old nerf loops reached x 0.56 at the tyres' z and ran through them; the Blaster's are plates on a tubular frame out of the lower rail between the tyres (z -0.27 to 0.19, x 0.2 to 0.5), the toothed pegs (fixed points) standing on them, a heel loop behind. The bumper is a silver tube loop at z 1.0 tied back to the frame's lower rail corner, with a skid plate under the nose.
  - **Proportion.** Length +4% and height +3% against the real figures over the wheelbase, tyre +3%, all inside 8%; `yellow` is **not** in `IN_PROPORTION`: the width is +10% and the track +9%, and both come from the layout every quad shares (wheels at x 0.48, 0.96 track against the real Blaster's 790/1115 = 0.71 of the wheelbase, 0.77 here), not from the body, so no body can fix them. It would take a per-vehicle wheel layout (physics, footrests, the girls' fixed points) or a narrower shared one: Jamie's call. The old model was 18% long and 15% wide.
  - **No fixed point moved** (`tests/fixedPoints.json` unchanged): the seat group's origin, the pillion grips on the strap, pegs, handlebars, grips, dash, anchors. The strap and pad on the seat are the old sport quad's.
  - Looked at in headless renders (seed 4): both sides, front and rear three-quarters, the front, from above and the engine bay at close range. Not looked at: lettering up close, the girls on it, night.
  - **Jamie's first round of changes** (same item): (1) the body was still too big, the rear wings most, and too horizontal: it is now a wedge, the front fenders lower (top 0.35 over the wheel centre), the rear wings shorter (z from 0.22 to -0.30 round the wheel, width 0.19 to 0.56) peaking behind the tyre's middle, the nose and tank sloping down to the front and the tail rising to 0.84. (2) The headlamp is not on the nose but a black housing on two brackets from the bar clamp, so it steers with the bars (`addBlasterBars`; the lens is still named `headlight` for the beams). (3) The bars rise in a V from the clamp (the green's V, `vBars` in `buildHandlebars`, through the grips' fixed point), with a hoop of uprights on the arms and a level crossbar joining them across the top, and the lever perches sit lower on the V. (4) The exhaust left the wing: the belly goes out to the right as before, then in at the front of the wing and under the side plate to a can inside the frame under the tail, ending before the tail's cross-tube; its metal and can are two-sided and the can has a dark baffle, so looking into it is not seeing through. The side plates dropped under the wings and below the frame's upper rail, which had passed through them. (5) The key is on a stalk from the clamp block, so its housing is anchored. (6) A brake cable from the left lever's perch down the column and into the nose, with the throttle's on the right (the upper parts in the bars' frame, the lower in the body's). Tests check the pipe is under the wing where it is beneath it, the can is inboard of the wing's inner edge and under the tail, the lamp is a child of the bars, the stalk touches the key's housing and the cables are there.
  - **Jamie's second round** (same item), from `.scratch/quads/tamura-blaster/handlebars.jpg` (the bar on its own): the lamp sits straight on the bars' middle (a small mount off the clamp block, no brackets); the bars run level across the middle and then rise steeply (about 50 degrees, `BLASTER_VBARS`, `barAt`) to the grips' fixed point, with a straight level crossbar joining the arms at x 0.25 and no hoop above them (and no pad on it, as in the photo); the lever perches and the throttle housing moved out to x 0.29, on the rise and outboard of the crossbar, and the brake lever is 0.85 of the usual length so its tip stays inside the grip's end. The key's stalk is gone (it was 0.2 m of it, because the V's middle is 7 cm under the bars' origin where the dash had stayed): the key's small housing now sits on the clamp block behind the lamp. **A fixed point moved on purpose:** the yellow's `dash`, from (0, 1.08, 0.5) to (0, 0.966, 0.44), 11 cm down and 6 cm back, since there was nothing for it to stand on up there. `tests/fixedPoints.json` regenerated for the yellow only (one line changed).
  - **Lights switch and the pull-start's new key** (same item, Jamie's ask). The yellow had no way to turn its lamp off. It gets a plain on / off rocker on the left bar, on `P` like every other vehicle with lights (`barLights: 'switch'` in its spec; `LightSwitch` takes the positions it has, `SIMPLE_LIGHT_MODES` being off and dipped, so the debug panel and the console list only those; `poseBarPod` rocks a `lightsRocker` that has no stop rocker or start button beside it). It starts on, as the others start dipped. But `P` was the two-strokes' pull-start, held, so the two would have fired together. Options considered: tap for lights and hold for the cord (a tap while feeling for the cord would flick the lights), `U` (the blue's diff lock, a key no two-stroke uses), or `O`. **The recoil pull-start is on `O` now** (`PULL_KEY` in `quad.js`, the gamepad ring's "Pull-start", the key editor, the help, the FAQ, the README): `O` is the tractor's stop knob and, in the toolbox panel, "take the pod off", and neither is reachable while you are at a two-stroke's cord. `P` stays the Series' starting handle from the front on foot (that one is not a pull-start key clash: its lights are only from the seat) and the toolbox's drain, as before. The girls' pulls (`helperPull`) never used a key.
  - **Wings over the tyres** (same item, Jamie's last note): the tyres were poking through the wings, by up to 8 cm at the front and 12 cm at the rear, mostly through the rolled outer lip (13 cm deep at the edge) and the low middle of the old profiles. Fixed by raising the wings, not moving them out, and cutting what hung down past the tyre: the lip is 5.5 cm at the edge, the front fender's peak 0.43 over the wheel centre (was 0.35) and the rear's 0.47 (was 0.44), the front fender runs from z 0.27 to -0.27 round the wheel (was 0.34 to -0.31) and the rear from 0.24 to -0.31. Measured on the real shell geometry (every vertex over the tyre's width against the tyre's 0.3 radius, `tests/blasterBody.test.js`): at least 3.9 cm of clearance at the front and 4.7 cm at the rear, for the tread's blocks and a little suspension travel. The cost is height: the fenders stand about 7 cm taller than before, and the wedge from low nose to high tail is shallower for it.
  - **Nose and tank lowered** (Jamie's call, to win the wedge back after the wings went up): the nose is 3 to 6 cm lower along its length (its face 0.44 to 0.49 high, was 0.48 to 0.55) with the vent lowered to match, and the tank's front slopes steeper (at z 0.5 it is 0.64 to 0.82, at its nose 0.63 to 0.75), the top at the cap unchanged.
  - **Nose raised again at the shocks, and widened** (Jamie: the front had dropped beneath the front springs and frame bars and stood apart from the wings): the lowering above took the nose under the shocks' tops (0.68 at x 0.15), so the springs stood out through its top, and its sides stopped 2 cm short of the wings' inner edges (0.22). Now at the shocks (z 0.55) it is 0.52 to 0.715 high and 0.46 wide, 0.44 to 0.62 and 0.52 wide at z 0.8, so the shocks' tops are inside it and the springs show below its front edge as in the photo, and it reaches 4 cm under the wings' inner edges. The face (z 0.93 on) and the vent stay as lowered. `tests/blasterBody.test.js` checks both.
- **The orange quad as a Suzuki LT250R Quadracer (TODO 92, done).** From seven images Jamie put in `.scratch/quads/viper-250/` (a brochure side view, front and rear three-quarters, two from above and the left switch housing). Answers up front: orange plastics with a **blue seat and blue frame tube** (the real one is yellow and blue); a lights switch as the yellow's (a plain on / off rocker, `barLights: 'switch'`, on `P`), so the orange's `noHeadlight` is gone and its lamp shines; **no number plates at all** (the round one on the nose and the two on the sides are gone); no hand guards; the choke is **on the controls**, a thumb lever on the pod on the left bar (`chokeStyle: 'thumb'`; it was a dash knob), not on the carb; the name is the **Viper Adder 250** (Viper is the orange's alone now). Jamie's brief: it has to look different from the others, as the Suzuki is a triangle-style wedge.
  - **The wedge** (`racerBody.js`, a body style of its own, `racer`; the old `sport2` stays in the code, unused). From above it is a triangle: a narrow nose and tank shroud (0.26 wide at the front, 0.38 at the bars), a seat that widens from 0.24 to 0.34 towards the back and a broad duck-tail between wings that reach out over the rear tyres. From the side the shroud stands high behind the bars (0.92) and falls away to a low blunt front with three louvres on its slope and the radiator's grille on its face, the seat is low (0.875), and the rear wings kick up behind it (0.60 over the wheel centre at their back against 0.30 at their front). The flanks are big flat triangles (a front top at the shroud, a rear top at the seat and an apex hanging low, `RACER_FLANK`), with the maker's badge on them; behind them a plate under each wing closes the side. The front fenders are flat blades over the tyres. Every wing is inside the tyre beside it (fronts 0.585, rears 0.60, against tyres to 0.59 and 0.625) and clear of it by 5 cm (a test measures the real shell geometry, as the yellow's).
  - **Parts shared with the yellow:** the V bars with a level crossbar (`BLASTER_VBARS`), the bumper (blue tube) and the wing shells' builder (`blasterFenders` takes the numbers). The bars have a pad on the crossbar (the photos' bar pad) and a square lamp on the clamp at the middle in a wire guard, so it steers with the bars. The frame is blue: every tube the shared builders made in the black frame material is swapped for `racerBlue()` after the nerf bars are built (the first try left the nerf bars black).
  - **Pod on the left bar** (`addRacerBars`, from the housing photo): a black housing on the level run with a yellow lights rocker (`lightsRocker`, found by `poseBarPod`'s plain-switch branch), the choke lever (`chokeLever`, the thumb style's own lever and pose, standing on the pod) and, on its right end, the kill switch's panel with its lanyard (the dash). **A fixed point moved on purpose:** the orange's `dash` from (0.225, 1.085, 0.425) to (0.195, 0.978, 0.43), 11 cm down, because on V bars the old place floated 13 cm above the bar; `tests/fixedPoints.json` regenerated for the orange only (one line).
  - **Exhaust:** as the yellow's (it had gone through the wing the same way): the belly out to the right, then in under the flank and the wing to a short silver silencer inside the frame under the tail, two-sided metal with a dark baffle in the can.
  - **Proportion:** not in `IN_PROPORTION`, and not fixable in the body: the shared layout makes the width and track about 10% over and the tyres 15% tall against the Suzuki's, and the length is about 10% over with the wedge's tail and bumper. Written up as README TODO item 344 (Jamie's ask) with the yellow's, since both want a per-vehicle wheel layout.
  - Looked at in headless renders (seed 4): the side, front and rear three-quarters, from above and the bars. Not looked at: the left pod's lettering up close, the girls on it, night.
  - **Jamie's first round of changes** (same item): (1) the rear wings are toned down: they end at the tyre's back (z -0.30 round the wheel, was -0.36), rise to 0.565 at the back (was 0.66), are 0.55 out (was 0.60) and the tail's top is 0.93 (was 0.95). (2) The choke lever went through the crossbar: the crossbar passes over the front of the pod, where the lever stood. Everything on the pod now stands on its rear half, behind the crossbar (the pod is 0.10 deep, the lever, the rocker and the dash's panel at z -0.075), and a test swings the lever through its whole travel against the crossbar. **The orange's dash moved again** for that (its fixed point is now (0.195, 0.978, 0.395); `tests/fixedPoints.json`, one line). (3) The frame did not join at the back: the lower rails ended in the air at z -0.98 (the frame builder only puts a cross-tube on rails that end forward of -0.95); the orange now ends them at -0.93 like the yellow, with the cross-tube. (4) **The thumb throttle:** the yellow and the orange had only a static plastic housing and paddle (the moving one, `buildThumbThrottle`, was the red's, the blue's and the green's). They now have it: a housing on the right bar's rise that is also the right brake lever's perch (the separate perch was under it), and a ribbed paddle that moves with the throttle (`poseThumbThrottle`), sitting on the V bar by an offset and an x. The bars' own black parts (the paddle) stay black on the orange; the blue swap leaves the handlebars alone.
- **The kids' quad as a Suzuki LT50 (TODO 93, done).** From three photos Jamie put in `.scratch/quads/viper-50/` (a brochure three-quarter, a yellow and blue one from the side and a white and blue one on grass). The blending was the frame's front tubes and head block sitting inside the swept path of the turning legs, boots, flap and fender (all of which turn about the bars' vertical axis, so the fork's parts never move against each other, only against the static frame): the cradle and spine now stop at z 0.25, and only a head tube on the steering axis itself, with a V of tubes back to the spines, reaches the steering. `barLights: 'switch'` gives the lights rocker (`P`, on or off, starting on, as the yellow's) on the left bar by the grip, and the thumb throttle (`buildThumbThrottle`, as the red's, yellow's and kids') is on the right, the brake and clutch perches moved in to 0.2.
